4.1.2 ReflectionandTransmission Now let us consider an infinitely long transmission line made of two semi-infinite trans- missionlinesofdifferentcharacteristicimpedancesthatarejoinedatz=0(Fig.4.2).The characteristic impedance is Z for z<0 and Z for z>0. Assume that there is a wave 01 02 propagating along the +z direction from the z<0 side. When this wave hits the joint, a portion of its power will be reflected and the remaining portion will be transmitted and continuetopropagatealongthedirection.Thetotalvoltagecanbewrittenas a e−𝛾z+Γa e𝛾z z<0 + + V(z)= (4.1.22)

Ta e−𝛾z z>0 Z Z 01 02 Figure4.2 Reflectionbyandtransmissionthroughajointbetweentwosemi-infinitetransmission lines.

Z Z 0 L Figure4.3 Reflectiononatransmissionlineterminatedbyanimpedanceload.

wherea denotestheamplitudeoftheincidentwave,Γdenotesthereflectioncoefficient, andT denotesthetransmissioncoefficient.Thecorrespondingcurrentisgivenby { / (a e−𝛾z−Γa e𝛾z) Z z<0 I(z)= + +/ 01 (4.1.23)

Ta e−𝛾z Z z>0.

+ 02 Since the voltages and currents on both sides of the joint are the same, we obtain two equations,whichcanbesolvedforΓandT,yielding Z Γ=， T =。 (4.1.24)

Next, we consider a uniform transmission line terminated by an impedance load Z_L at z=z_0 (Fig.4.3). In this case, the voltage and current on the transmission line can be written as the superposition of the incident and reflected waves: V(z) = a_+ e^{-𝛾z} + Γ a_+ e^{𝛾z} (4.1.25)

I(z) = (a_+ e^{-𝛾z} - Γ a_+ e^{𝛾z}) / Z_0. (4.1.26)

At z=z_0, the voltage and current are related by V(z_0) / I(z_0) = Z_L, from which we find Γ = (Z_L - Z_0) / (Z_L + Z_0) e^{-2𝛾z_0}. (4.1.27)

If we define an impedance at z as Z(z) = V(z) / I(z), by substituting Equations (4.1.25) and (4.1.26) into this definition, we have Z(z) = Z_0 (1 + Γ e^{2𝛾z}) / (1 - Γ e^{2𝛾z}) = Z_0 (1 + Γ(z)) / (1 - Γ(z)) (4.1.28)

where Γ(z) = Γ e^{2𝛾z} is the reflection coefficient observed at z. If we further make use of Equation (4.1.27), we find that Z(z) = Z_0 (Z_L cosh 𝛾l + Z_0 sinh 𝛾l) / (Z_0 cosh 𝛾l + Z_L sinh 𝛾l) (4.1.29)

where l = z - z_0. For a lossless transmission line, 𝛾 = j𝛽, and this is reduced to Z(z) = Z_0 (Z_L cos 𝛽l + j Z_0 sin 𝛽l) / (Z_0 cos 𝛽l + j Z_L sin 𝛽l). (4.1.30)

Clearly, the value of this impedance depends on the point of observation and the load at the terminal. There are three special cases, which deserve to be mentioned explicitly. (1) When Z_L = Z_0, Γ = 0 and Z(z) = Z_0. (2) When Z_L = 0 (short circuit), Γ(z_0) = -1 and Z(z) = j Z_0 tan 𝛽l. (3) When Z_L = ∞ (open circuit), Γ(z_0) = 1 and Z(z) = -j Z_0 cot 𝛽l. It is interesting to note that when 𝛽l = 𝜋/2 or l = 𝜆/4, a short circuit is transformed into an open circuit and an open circuit is transformed into a short circuit.

Our discussion here concerns only the basic concepts and characteristics of transmission lines, which are similar to those of plane waves. An extensive coverage of transmission line theory can be found in the book by Miner [1], which includes the analysis of a variety of transmission lines for practical applications.

4.1.3 Green’s Function and Eigenfunction Expansion Consider a transmission line with a distributed current source i(z), which has a unit of amperes per unit length (Fig. 4.4). In this case, Equation (4.1.1) remains the same and Equation (4.1.2) becomes dI/dz + (j𝜔C + G) V = i(z). (4.1.31)

The second-order differential equation for V becomes d²V/dz² - 𝛾²V = -(j𝜔L + R) i(z). (4.1.32)

Because Equation (4.1.32) is a linear equation, by linear superposition its solution can be written as V(z) = (j𝜔L + R) ∫_{z_1}^{z_2} g(z, z′) i(z′) dz′ (4.1.33)

where it is assumed that the source i(z) is confined within the region [z_1, z_2] and g(z, z′) is often referred to as the Green’s function. By substituting Equation (4.1.33) into Equation (4.1.32) and noting that i(z) = ∫_{z_1}^{z_2} 𝛿(z - z′) i(z′) dz′ (4.1.34)

we obtain the second-order differential equation for g(z, z′) as d²g(z, z′)/dz² - 𝛾²g(z, z′) = -𝛿(z - z′). (4.1.35)

Since 𝛿(z - z′) = 0 for z < z′ and z > z′, the solution in these two regions in the case of an infinitely long transmission line is given by g_0(z, z′) = { A e^{𝛾z} for z < z′, B e^{-𝛾z} for z > z′ } (4.1.36)

where A and B are unknown constants. If the transmission line is finite, we need to add the terms representing reflected waves, whose coefficients can be determined by the impedance load at the terminals. Since g_0(z, z′) is related to the voltage, which must be continuous in the absence of a voltage source, g_0(z, z′)|_{z=z′+0} = g_0(z, z′)|_{z=z′-0}. (4.1.37)

Now by integrating Equation (4.1.35) over a small interval around z′, we obtain dg_0(z, z′)/dz|_{z=z′+0} - dg_0(z, z′)/dz|_{z=z′-0} = -1. (4.1.38)

Applying both Equations (4.1.37) and (4.1.38) to Equation (4.1.36), we can determine both A and B and thus the specific form of g_0(z, z′) as g_0(z, z′) = { (1/2𝛾) e^{𝛾(z - z′)} for z ≤ z′, (1/2𝛾) e^{-𝛾(z - z′)} for z ≥ z′ }. (4.1.39)

With this Green’s function, the voltage due to any distributed current source can be obtained simply by evaluating the integral in Equation (4.1.33).

The method to derive g_0(z, z′) described earlier is very similar to the approach described in Section 2.2.2. In the following, we introduce another approach, which can be extended systematically to other problems. In this approach, we expand both g_0(z, z′) and 𝛿(z - z′) using the Fourier transform as g_0(z, z′) = (1/2𝜋) ∫_{-∞}^{∞} f(h) e^{jhz} dh (4.1.40)

𝛿(z - z′) = (1/2𝜋) ∫_{-∞}^{∞} e^{jh(z - z′)} dh. (4.1.41)

Substituting these two expressions into Equation (4.1.35), we obtain f(h) = e^{-jhz′} / (h² + 𝛾²) (4.1.42)

and when this is substituted into Equation (4.1.40), we have g_0(z, z′) = (1/2𝜋) ∫_{-∞}^{∞} e^{jh(z - z′)} / (h² + 𝛾²) dh. (4.1.43)

The integral can be evaluated using Cauchy’s residue theorem [2]. First, we observe that the integrand has two poles, h = ±j𝛾 (Fig.4.5), and the contour integral is along the real axis and closed at infinity. To obtain a physically meaningful solution, the contour integral for the case of z - z′ < 0 has to be closed in the lower half-plane, and for the case of z - z′ > 0 it has to be closed in the upper half-plane. The result of integration is g_0(z, z′) = { (1/2𝛾) e^{𝛾(z - z′)} for z ≤ z′, (1/2𝛾) e^{-𝛾(z - z′)} for z ≥ z′ } (4.1.44)

which is identical to Equation (4.1.39).

Compared to the first approach, the second approach to deriving g_0(z, z′) is more systematic. Although the second approach is based on the concept of Fourier transform, Equation (4.1.40) can be interpreted as expanding g_0(z, z′) in terms of eigenfunctions e^{jhz} with h as the eigenvalues. These eigenfunctions are the solution of the homogeneous equation d²f/dz² + h²f = 0. (4.1.45)

Hence, this approach can be called the method of eigenfunction expansion [3], which can be used systematically to derive Green’s functions for more complicated problems. To show this, let us rederive the three-dimensional free-space Green’s function, which is the solution of Equation (2.2.14). We first expand G_0(𝐫, 𝐫′) and 𝛿(𝐫 - 𝐫′) in terms of Fourier integrals as G_0(𝐫, 𝐫′) = (1/(2𝜋)³) ∫∫∫ A(h_x, h_y, h_z) e^{j(h_x x + h_y y + h_z z)} dh_x dh_y dh_z = (1/(2𝜋)³) ∫∫∫ A(𝐡) e^{j𝐡⋅𝐫} d𝐡 (4.1.46)

𝛿(𝐫 - 𝐫′) = (1/(2𝜋)³) ∫∫∫ e^{j[h_x (x - x′) + h_y (y - y′) + h_z (z - z′)]} dh_x dh_y dh_z = (1/(2𝜋)³) ∫∫∫ e^{j𝐡⋅(𝐫 - 𝐫′)} d𝐡 (4.1.47)

where A(𝐡) is the unknown expansion coefficient to be determined. By substituting these two expressions into Equation (2.2.14), we obtain A(𝐡) = e^{-j𝐡⋅𝐫′} / (h² - k²) (4.1.48)

where h² = h_x² + h_y² + h_z². Hence, G_0(𝐫, 𝐫′) = (1/(2𝜋)³) ∫∫∫ e^{j𝐡⋅(𝐫 - 𝐫′)} / (h² - k²) d𝐡 (4.1.49)

which is called the spectral representation of the three-dimensional free-space Green’s function. To evaluate the spectral integrals, we convert the integrals in rectangular coordinates into those in spherical coordinates by letting h_x = h sin𝜃 cos𝜙, h_y = h sin𝜃 sin𝜙, h_z = h cos𝜃 (4.1.50)

so that d𝐡 becomes d𝐡 = h² sin𝜃 dh d𝜃 d𝜙. (4.1.51)

Furthermore, because of the spherical symmetry of G_0(𝐫, 𝐫′) with respect to the point 𝐫′, the value of G_0(𝐫, 𝐫′) is independent of the direction of 𝐫 - 𝐫′. Therefore, we can choose an arbitrary 𝐫 - 𝐫′ for the evaluation of G_0(𝐫, 𝐫′). If we choose the direction of 𝐫 - 𝐫′ to coincide with the z-direction, Equation (4.1.49) can be written as G_0(𝐫, 𝐫′) = (1/(2𝜋)²) ∫_{0}^{∞} (h²/(h² - k²)) e^{jh cos𝜃 |𝐫 - 𝐫′|} sin𝜃 d𝜃 dh = (j/(2𝜋)²|𝐫 - 𝐫′|) ∫_{0}^{∞} (h/(h² - k²)) (e^{-jh|𝐫 - 𝐫′|} - e^{jh|𝐫 - 𝐫′|}) dh = (j/(2𝜋)²|𝐫 - 𝐫′|) ∫_{-∞}^{∞} (h e^{-jh|𝐫 - 𝐫′|} / (h² - k²)) dh. (4.1.52)

This integral can now be evaluated using Cauchy’s residue theorem. The integrand has two poles: h = ±k. Although the problem considered here is lossless, we can treat it as a limiting case of a lossy problem. Consequently, the pole at h = k is on the lower side of the real axis and the pole at h = -k is on the upper side of the real axis. To have a physically meaningful solution, the contour integral must be closed in the lower half-plane, as shown in Figure 4.6. Applying Cauchy’s residue theorem, we obtain G_0(𝐫, 𝐫′) = e^{-jk|𝐫 - 𝐫′|} / (4𝜋|𝐫 - 𝐫′|) (4.1.53)

which is the same as Equation (2.2.20), even though the derivation is completely different.

4.2 WAVE EQUATIONS AND GENERAL SOLUTIONS

After the brief review of transmission line theory, we now turn our attention to the propagation of electromagnetic waves. In Chapter 2, we discovered that the electromagnetic fields produced in the far-field zone by any finite time-harmonic sources propagate along the radial direction in the form of e^{-jkr}. To understand the nature of electromagnetic wave propagation, we now consider a special wave, called a uniform plane wave, and examine its basic properties.

4.2.1 Wave Equations and Solution by Separation of Variables Consider a homogeneous and source-free medium, where time-harmonic electromagnetic fields satisfy source-free Maxwell’s equations ∇ × 𝐄 = -j𝜔𝜇𝐇, ∇ × 𝐇 = j𝜔𝜖𝐄 + 𝜎𝐄 (4.2.1)

from which we find that both 𝐄 and 𝐇 satisfy the same vector Helmholtz equation ∇²𝐄 - 𝛾²𝐄 = 0, ∇²𝐇 - 𝛾²𝐇 = 0 (4.2.2)

where 𝛾² = j𝜔𝜇(j𝜔𝜖 + 𝜎). Each component of 𝐄 and 𝐇 satisfies the same scalar Helmholtz equation, which for E_x can be written as ∇²E_x - 𝛾²E_x = 0 (4.2.3)

or ∂²E_x/∂x² + ∂²E_x/∂y² + ∂²E_x/∂z² - 𝛾²E_x = 0. (4.2.4)

The solution to this equation can be sought using the method of separation of variables. In this method, we first assume that E_x can be represented as the product of three functions: E_x = X(x) Y(y) Z(z). (4.2.5)

When this is substituted into Equation (4.2.4), we obtain (1/X) d²X/dx² + (1/Y) d²Y/dy² + (1/Z) d²Z/dz² - 𝛾² = 0 (4.2.6)

after being divided by XYZ. Since the first three terms are independent of one another, they have to be constant, or in other words, Equation (4.2.6) can be separated into three equations (1/X) d²X/dx² = 𝛾_x², (1/Y) d²Y/dy² = 𝛾_y², (1/Z) d²Z/dz² = 𝛾_z² (4.2.7)

where 𝛾_x², 𝛾_y², and 𝛾_z² are arbitrary constants that are constrained by 𝛾_x² + 𝛾_y² + 𝛾_z² = 𝛾². The solutions to these three equations are given by X = A_x e^{±𝛾_x x}, Y = A_y e^{±𝛾_y y}, Z = A_z e^{±𝛾_z z} (4.2.8)

and hence the solution to E_x is given by E_x = A e^{±𝛾_x x ± 𝛾_y y ± � For simplicity, 𝐄 is assumed to be a real vector here. Since cos(𝜔t±𝜷⋅𝐫) represents a wave propagating along the ∓𝛽̂ direction whose phase is constant in the plane defined by 𝜷⋅𝐫 = constant, e±𝜸⋅𝐫 represents a plane wave. The amplitude of the wave is also uniform in the plane defined by 𝜶⋅𝐫 = constant. In the case that 𝜶 and 𝜷 have the same direction, the equiphase and equiamplitude planes are parallel to each other, and the wave is called a uniform plane wave. If 𝜶 and 𝜷 have different directions, the wave is called a nonuniform plane wave [4].

Since Equations (4.2.11) and (4.2.12) are valid for any 𝛾x and 𝛾y, the general solution to Equation (4.2.2) is the linear superposition of all possible solutions, which can be expressed as $$ \mathbf{E}(\mathbf{r}) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \mathbf{E}_0(\gamma_x, \gamma_y) e^{\pm \boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \mathbf{r}} d\gamma_x d\gamma_y \quad (4.2.14)

$$ $$ \mathbf{H}(\mathbf{r}) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \mathbf{H}_0(\gamma_x, \gamma_y) e^{\pm \boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \mathbf{r}} d\gamma_x d\gamma_y. \quad (4.2.15)

$$ Note that 𝛾z is constrained by 𝛾x²+𝛾y²+𝛾z² = 𝛾². Equations (4.2.14) and (4.2.15) indicate that any field at a source-free point can be expanded as a linear superposition of an infinite number of plane waves.

4.2.2 Characteristics of a Plane Wave Let us consider the plane wave represented by Equations (4.2.11) and (4.2.12) further and study its fundamental characteristics. For this, we note that for an exponential function in the form of e^{𝜸⋅𝐫}, it can be verified easily that $$ \nabla e^{\boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \mathbf{r}} = \boldsymbol{\gamma} e^{\boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \mathbf{r}}, \quad \nabla \cdot \hat{\mathbf{a}} e^{\boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \mathbf{r}} = \boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \hat{\mathbf{a}} e^{\boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \mathbf{r}}, \quad \nabla \times \hat{\mathbf{a}} e^{\boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \mathbf{r}} = \boldsymbol{\gamma} \times \hat{\mathbf{a}} e^{\boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \mathbf{r}} \quad (4.2.16)

$$ where 𝐚̂ is an arbitrary constant unit vector. By substituting Equations (4.2.11) and (4.2.12) into Maxwell’s equations in Equation (4.2.1) and with the aid of Equation (4.2.16), we obtain that for a plane wave, $$ \pm \boldsymbol{\gamma} \times \mathbf{E} = -j\omega\mu\mathbf{H}, \quad \pm \boldsymbol{\gamma} \times \mathbf{H} = (j\omega\epsilon + \sigma)\mathbf{E}. \quad (4.2.17)

$$ It is obvious that 𝜸, 𝐄, and 𝐇 are perpendicular to each other, that is, $$ \boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \mathbf{E} = 0, \quad \boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \mathbf{H} = 0, \quad \mathbf{E} \cdot \mathbf{H} = 0 \quad (4.2.18)

$$ and the first two are nothing but the results of the two Maxwell’s divergence equations. The wave impedance, which is the ratio of the electric field to the magnetic field, is given by $$ Z_w = \frac{|\mathbf{E}|}{|\mathbf{H}|} = \frac{\gamma}{j\omega\epsilon + \sigma} = \frac{j\omega\mu}{j\omega\epsilon + \sigma} \quad (4.2.19)

$$ which is the same as the intrinsic impedance of the medium, often denoted as 𝜂. Similar to the characteristic impedance of a transmission line, the intrinsic impedance of a medium depends only on the property of the medium itself. However, the wave impedance depends on a specific electromagnetic wave. For a uniform plane wave, the wave impedance has the same value as the intrinsic impedance; for other types of wave it may not. By taking the cross-product of the first equation in Equation (4.2.17) with 𝜸 and then invoking the second equation, we obtain $$ \boldsymbol{\gamma} \times (\boldsymbol{\gamma} \times \mathbf{E}) = -j\omega\mu(j\omega\epsilon + \sigma)\mathbf{E} \quad (4.2.20)

$$ which can further be reduced to $$ (\boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \boldsymbol{\gamma}) \mathbf{E} = j\omega\mu(j\omega\epsilon + \sigma)\mathbf{E} \quad (4.2.21)

$$ since 𝜸×(𝜸×𝐄) = (𝜸⋅𝐄)𝜸 − (𝜸⋅𝜸)𝐄 = −(𝜸⋅𝜸)𝐄. Because 𝐄 ≠ 0, we have $$ \boldsymbol{\gamma} \cdot \boldsymbol{\gamma} = \gamma_x^2 + \gamma_y^2 + \gamma_z^2 = \gamma^2 = j\omega\mu(j\omega\epsilon + \sigma) \quad (4.2.22)

$$ which is consistent with the constraint obtained from the method of separation of variables. This equation is called the dispersion relation, which relates the propagation constant to the property of the medium.

The results derived before are pertinent to a lossy medium in which 𝜸 is a complex vector. Since a complex vector is difficult to visualize, let us specialize to a lossless medium in which 𝜸 = j𝜷 and 𝜷 is a real vector. As shown earlier, e^{−j𝜷⋅𝐫} represents a plane wave propagating in the 𝛽̂ direction. In this case, Equation (4.2.17) becomes $$ \boldsymbol{\beta} \times \mathbf{E} = \omega\mu\mathbf{H}, \quad \boldsymbol{\beta} \times \mathbf{H} = -\omega\epsilon\mathbf{E} \quad (4.2.23)

$$ from which we have 𝜷⋅𝐄 = 0, 𝜷⋅𝐇 = 0, and 𝐄⋅𝐇 = 0. In addition, it can be found easily that 𝜔𝜇𝐄×𝐇 = E₀²𝜷, which indicates that the electric field vector, the magnetic field vector, and the propagation vector format a triad and obey the right-hand rule. Therefore, given any two vectors, we can easily determine the third by using our right hand. The wave impedance now becomes $$ Z_w = \frac{|\mathbf{E}|}{|\mathbf{H}|} = \frac{\omega\mu}{\beta} = \frac{\beta}{\omega\epsilon} = \sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\epsilon}} \quad (4.2.24)

$$ and the dispersion relation becomes $$ \beta_x^2 + \beta_y^2 + \beta_z^2 = \beta^2 = \omega^2\mu\epsilon. \quad (4.2.25)

$$ As in the transmission line theory, 𝛽 is called the phase constant or wavenumber.

4.2.3 Wave Velocities and Attenuation Consider a uniform plane wave with the instantaneous electric field given by Equation (4.2.13). Along the propagation direction 𝐫 = 𝛽̂r, cos(𝜔t−𝜷⋅𝐫) becomes cos(𝜔t−𝛽r); hence, the phase velocity of the wave is given by $$ v_p = \frac{\omega}{\beta}. \quad (4.2.26)

$$ In a lossless medium, 𝛽 = 𝜔√(𝜇𝜖); therefore, 𝑣p = 1/√(𝜇𝜖). In vacuum, 𝜖 = 𝜖₀ and 𝜇 = 𝜇₀, and thus the phase velocity is given by $$ v_p = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu_0 \epsilon_0}} \approx 2.997925 \times 10^8 \, \text{m/s} \quad (4.2.27)

$$ which is the same as the speed of light.

The phase velocity measures the motion of a constant phase plane. Since electromagnetic waves carry energy, the motion of the energy is described by the energy velocity, which is defined as $$ v_e = \frac{\text{power flow density}}{\text{energy density}} = \frac{\mathbf{S}}{w_e + w_m}. \quad (4.2.28)

$$ For a uniform plane wave in a lossless medium, the instantaneous magnetic field is given by $$ \mathbf{H}(\mathbf{r}, t) = \text{Re} \left[ \mathbf{H}_0 e^{j\omega t - j\boldsymbol{\beta} \cdot \mathbf{r}} \right] = \mathbf{H}_0 \cos(\omega t - \boldsymbol{\beta} \cdot \mathbf{r}) = \frac{\boldsymbol{\beta} \times \mathbf{E}_0}{\omega\mu} \cos(\omega t - \boldsymbol{\beta} \cdot \mathbf{r}). \quad (4.2.29)

$$ Thus, the instantaneous power flow density is $$ \mathbf{S} = \mathbf{E} \times \mathbf{H} = \boldsymbol{\beta} \frac{|\mathbf{E}_0|^2}{\omega\mu} \cos^2(\omega t - \boldsymbol{\beta} \cdot \mathbf{r}) \quad (4.2.30)

$$ which flows in the propagation direction 𝛽̂. The instantaneous electric and magnetic energy densities are given by $$ w_e = \frac{1}{2} \epsilon |\mathbf{E}|^2 = \frac{1}{2} \epsilon |\mathbf{E}_0|^2 \cos^2(\omega t - \boldsymbol{\beta} \cdot \mathbf{r}) \quad (4.2.31)

$$ $$ w_m = \frac{1}{2} \mu |\mathbf{H}|^2 = \frac{1}{2} \epsilon |\mathbf{E}_0|^2 \cos^2(\omega t - \boldsymbol{\beta} \cdot \mathbf{r}) \quad (4.2.32)

$$ which are equal to each other. When these are substituted into Equation (4.2.28), we obtain the energy velocity in a lossless medium as $$ v_e = \frac{1}{\eta\epsilon} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu\epsilon}} \quad (4.2.33)

$$ which is the same as the phase velocity 𝑣p. Note that this is true only for a uniform plane wave and may not hold for other types of waves.

When we deal with propagation of electromagnetic waves used for communication, we often use group velocity to characterize the propagation of signals. To understand the concept of group velocity, we consider two waves propagating at two slightly different frequencies, whose combined electric field is given by $$ \mathbf{E}(\mathbf{r}, t) = \mathbf{E}_0 \cos[(\omega + \Delta\omega)t - (\boldsymbol{\beta} + \Delta\boldsymbol{\beta}) \cdot \mathbf{r}] + \mathbf{E}_0 \cos[(\omega - \Delta\omega)t - (\boldsymbol{\beta} - \Delta\boldsymbol{\beta}) \cdot \mathbf{r}] \\ = 2\mathbf{E}_0 \cos(\Delta\omega t - \Delta\boldsymbol{\beta} \cdot \mathbf{r}) \cos(\omega t - \boldsymbol{\beta} \cdot \mathbf{r}). \quad (4.2.34)

$$ The waveform along the propagation direction is plotted in Figure 4.7 for one instant of time. It is a product of two sinusoids: cos(Δ𝜔𝑡 − Δ𝜷⋅𝐫) and cos(𝜔𝑡 − 𝜷⋅𝐫). The second sinusoid propagates at the phase velocity given by Equation (4.2.26). The first sinusoid, which appears as the envelope of the wave and is often called a wave packet, moves at a different velocity, which is defined as the group velocity. Along the propagation direction 𝛽̂, this velocity is given by $$ v_g = \frac{\Delta\omega}{\Delta\beta} \quad \text{for} \quad \Delta\omega \rightarrow 0. \quad (4.2.35)

$$ Usually, 𝛽 can be expressed as the function of 𝜔; hence, the group velocity can be written as $$ v_g = \frac{\Delta\omega}{\Delta\beta} = \left( \frac{\Delta\beta}{\Delta\omega} \right)^{-1} = \frac{d\beta}{d\omega}^{-1}. \quad (4.2.36)

$$ In a lossless dispersion-free medium, 𝛽 = 𝜔√(𝜇𝜖) and both 𝜖 and 𝜇 are independent of frequency. The group velocity is then given by $$ v_g = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu\epsilon}} \quad (4.2.37)

$$ which is the same as the phase velocity and also the energy velocity. However, if 𝜖 and 𝜇 change with frequency, the phase velocity changes accordingly, and hence, $$ \frac{d\beta}{d\omega} = \frac{d}{d\omega} \left( \frac{\omega}{v_p} \right) = \frac{1}{v_p} - \frac{\omega}{v_p^2} \frac{dv_p}{d\omega}. \quad (4.2.38)

$$ The group velocity is then given by $$ v_g = \frac{v_p}{1 - \frac{\omega}{v_p} \frac{dv_p}{d\omega}} \quad (4.2.39)

$$ which shows clearly the relationship between the group and phase velocities. In a medium with no dispersion, 𝑑𝑣p/𝑑𝜔=0, then 𝑣g = 𝑣p. In a medium with normal dispersion, 𝑑𝑣p/𝑑𝜔<0, then 𝑣g < 𝑣p. On the other hand, in a medium with anomalous dispersion, 𝑑𝑣p/𝑑𝜔>0, then 𝑣g > 𝑣p. Therefore, we can control the group velocity by utilizing a dispersive medium, making it possible to design slow- and fast-wave devices. In many situations with normal dispersion, the group velocity is the same as the energy velocity [5].

In a medium with conduction loss (for simplicity we ignore dielectric and magnetic losses here), the propagation constant is given by 𝛾 = 𝛼 + j𝛽 = √[j𝜔𝜇(j𝜔𝜖+𝜎)], where $$ \alpha = \omega \sqrt{\frac{\mu\epsilon}{2} \left[ \sqrt{1 + \left( \frac{\sigma}{\omega\epsilon} \right)^2} - 1 \right]}, \quad \beta = \omega \sqrt{\frac{\mu\epsilon}{2} \left[ \sqrt{1 + \left( \frac{\sigma}{\omega\epsilon} \right)^2} + 1 \right]}. \quad (4.2.40)

$$ For a good dielectric, (𝜎/𝜔𝜖)²≪1; hence, Equation (4.2.40) and the intrinsic impedance can be approximated as $$ \alpha \approx \frac{\sigma}{2} \sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\epsilon}}, \quad \beta \approx \omega\sqrt{\mu\epsilon}, \quad \eta \approx \sqrt{\frac{\mu}{\epsilon}} \quad (4.2.41)

$$ whereas for a good conductor, (𝜎/𝜔𝜖)²≫1; thus, Equation (4.2.40) and the intrinsic impedance can be approximated as $$ \alpha \approx \sqrt{\frac{\omega\mu\sigma}{2}}, \quad \beta \approx \sqrt{\frac{\omega\mu\sigma}{2}}, \quad \eta \approx (1+j)\sqrt{\frac{\omega\mu}{2\sigma}}. \quad (4.2.42)

$$ These approximations are useful for engineering applications.

EXAMPLE 4.1 First, show that if the displacement current is neglected, the differential equation of the conduction current density (𝐉c = 𝜎𝐄, where 𝜎 is a constant) in a conductor is given by $$ \nabla^2 \mathbf{J}_c = \mu\sigma \frac{\partial \mathbf{J}_c}{\partial t}.

$$ Next, consider an infinite half-space (below the z=0 plane) having permeability 𝜇 and conductivity 𝜎 and carrying a sinusoidal (time-harmonic) current of angular frequency 𝜔 in the x-direction. Assume that |𝐉c| = 1 A/m² at z=0 and there is no variation along the y-direction. Using the differential equation obtained earlier, find the solution for the current density in the half-space.

Solution After neglecting the displacement current, Maxwell’s equations become $$ \nabla \times \mathbf{E} = -\mu \frac{\partial \mathbf{H}}{\partial t}, \quad \nabla \times \mathbf{H} = \mathbf{J}_c.

$$ Multiplying the first equation by 𝜎, we obtain $$ \nabla \times (\sigma\mathbf{E}) = -\mu\sigma \frac{\partial \mathbf{H}}{\partial t} \quad \text{or} \quad \nabla \times \mathbf{J}_c = -\mu\sigma \frac{\partial \mathbf{H}}{\partial t}.

$$ To eliminate 𝐇, we take the curl on this equation to obtain $$ \nabla \times (\nabla \times \mathbf{J}_c) = -\mu\sigma \frac{\partial}{\partial t} (\nabla \times \mathbf{H}) = -\mu\sigma \frac{\partial \mathbf{J}_c}{\partial t}.

$$ Using the vector identity ∇×(∇×𝐉c) = ∇(∇⋅𝐉c
